Sensitivity estimates for GPT-OSS-120B and Qwen 3.6-27B were similar, with overlapping confidence intervals. Specificity was 0.99 (95% CI 0.97-0.99) for Qwen 3.6-27B, 0.96 (0.91-0.99) for GPT-OSS-120B, and 0.95 (0.92-0.97) for Kimi K3. Kimi K3 also achieved the highest F1 score (0.87), compared with GPT-OSS-120B (0.78) and Qwen 3.6-27B (0.77). When discordant occurrences were included, the ranking of models was unchanged (sensitivity 0.78, 0.65, and 0.62 for Kimi K3, GPT-OSS-120B, and Qwen 3.6-27B, respectively; Supplemental §7, Table S7.1b).

3.2 Model Performance across clinical note sources

To examine whether the pipelines performed consistently across different forms of clinical documentation, we evaluated feature identification separately for Fung et al cases, PMC case reports, and MIMIC-IV notes (Table 3). Sensitivity varied across clinical note sources, although the relative performance of the three models remained consistent (Table 3). Kimi K3 achieved the highest sensitivity across all sources, ranging from 0.68 in Fung cases to 0.82 in PMC case reports. GPT-OSS-120B and Qwen 3.6-27B showed comparable, but consistently lower, sensitivity across sources. All three models demonstrated their lowest sensitivity in Fung cases, suggesting greater difficulty in recovering documented clinical features from these cases. Specificity remained high across clinical note sources (0.82-1.00), with several models achieving perfect specificity in subsets containing relatively few physician-confirmed absent features. Pooled across models, the median number of features correctly identified per note was 3 (IQR 2-4.75) for Fung et al cases, 6 (4-8) for PMC reports, and 7.5 (3-11) for MIMIC-IV notes (Supplemental §7, Table S7.3). *A specificity of 1.00 means no features were wrongly reported as present. This value derives from only a small number of features that the physicians marked absent, and should be interpreted as "no errors of commission seen in this case-model configuration" rather than as precise estimates of true specificity.

3.3 Composition of errors

To further characterize the differences in feature identification, we examined the types of disagreement between model outputs and physician assessments. Across all three models, most false negatives resulted from omissions, where clinically relevant features were not reported, rather than negated misses, where features were incorrectly marked as absent (Table 4). Omissions accounted for 94.9% of false negatives in Qwen 3.6-27B, 100% in GPT-OSS-120B, and 99.0% in Kimi K3. Kimi K3 had the fewest false negatives overall (n=196), compared with GPT-OSS-120B (n=317) and Qwen 3.6-27B (n=354). False positives were uncommon across all three models, with 2 for Qwen 3.6-27B, 4 for GPT-OSS-120B, and 12 for Kimi K3. Per case evaluation, the median number of false negatives was 3 (IQR 2-5) for Qwen 3.6-27B, 3 (1-4.25) for GPT-OSS-120B, and 1 (1-3) for Kimi K3, while false positives had a median of 0 and a maximum of 1 for all models (Figure 3). Among features reported as absent, physicians confirmed absence in 108 of 117 (92.3%) for Qwen 3.6-27B, 50 of 50 (100%) for GPT-OSS-120B, and 118 of 119 (99.2%) for Kimi K3. Frequently missed features included laboratory and definitional criteria such as "unexplained clinical illness consistent with infection" (missed in 10 of 11, 8 of 10, and 3 of 9 occurrences, respectively) (Supplemental §7, Tables S7.2a-d).

3.4 Quality of Extracted Information

Beyond identifying clinical features, we evaluated the accuracy of their associated timing and descriptive details. Descriptive details were extracted more accurately than timing information across all three models (Table 5). Under the strict definition, detail accuracy was highest for GPT-OSS-120B (93.5%), compared with Kimi K3 (92.4%) and Qwen 3.6-27B (91.2%). When reasonable inferences were included, Kimi K3 achieved the highest accuracy (98.2%), with slightly lower values for Qwen 3.6-27B (97.9%) and GPT-OSS-120B (97.4%). Timing accuracy was lower across models, with Kimi K3 achieving the highest rates under both strict (88.7%) and lenient (97.6%) definitions, compared with GPT-OSS-120B (88.4% and 95.3%) and Qwen 3.6-27B (81.8% and 92.7%). Inaccurate assessments accounted for at most 7.3% of timing evaluations and 2.6% of detail evaluations.

3.5 Model-Physician Agreement

To assess overall agreement between model outputs and physician assessments, we calculated Gwet's AC1, accounting for agreement expected by chance. Kimi K3 showed the highest chance-corrected agreement with physician reviewers (Gwet's AC1 0.68, 95% CI 0.63-0.72), with an observed agreement of 81.3%. Agreement was lower for GPT-OSS-120B (AC1 0.46, 95% CI 0.40-0.52) and Qwen 3.6-27B (AC1 0.44, 95% CI 0.39-0.50), with observed agreement of 67.1% and 69.3%, respectively. The confidence intervals for GPT-OSS-120B and Qwen 3.6-27B overlapped, whereas neither overlapped with that of Kimi K3.

4 | DISCUSSION

We developed and evaluated a multi-stage AI pipeline to extract standardized transfusion reaction features from three sources of free-text clinical notes and compared performance across three open-weight LLMs. Models achieved modest sensitivity, detecting about three-quarters of all features present, and very high specificity. The largest model (Kimi K3) generally yielded the best performance, and all models achieved higher sensitivity on published case reports (PMC) and real-world notes (MIMIC-IV) than on expert-written scenarios. Sensitivity was modest, with models discovering 2/3 to 3/4 of features, and models rarely reported features that were not present (specificity ≥0.95). Models were also able to extract additional details about the timing and clinical descriptions of reported features, with greater than 90% accuracy across all model/note combinations. Our results demonstrate the technical feasibility of clinical note extraction on a task that is typically time intensive and requires clinical expertise.

Several findings deserve nuanced interpretation. The modest sensitivity in our study partly reflects inherent complexity and potential overlap of reactions characteristics described in the NHSN. For instance, the pairs 'hematuria' and 'hemoglobinuria', and, 'acute or worsening respiratory distress' and 'respiratory distress', appear among the top 10 most commonly missed features, and some models extracted one feature but not the other, while human experts tended to code both as accurate or reasonable extractions (Supplemental §7). Prior work has also reported that LLMs may generate unsupported content when information is absent from the source document. [28] The high precision in our study suggests that restricting outputs to a closed vocabulary and requiring verbatim supporting evidence limited such errors. We found that Kimi K3 performed best, followed by GPT-OSS-120B and Qwen 3.6-27B, which is expected and follows the order of parameter size of these models. Contrary to our expectations, we found that models performed better on the most complex, but also most realistic, clinical notes (MIMIC-IV) than on the expert-written scenarios, potentially indicating that rich clinical context improves LLM performance.

Our work extends previous research, where multi-agent LLM pipelines have been applied to extract structured information from free-text clinical documentation, including radiology reports, pathology reports, and hospital discharge summaries. [13] GPT-4 extracted lesion information from lung cancer CT reports with 98.6% accuracy, [29] GPT-3.5 classified lung cancer pathology reports with 89% accuracy, [28] and a locally deployed open-weight model identified liver cirrhosis in MIMIC-IV medical histories with 100% sensitivity and 96% specificity. [30] Within transfusion medicine, automated surveillance has largely relied on rule-based electronic health record algorithms targeting individual reactions such as transfusion-related acute lung injury and transfusion-associated circulatory overload, [31] and recent work has evaluated LLMs on standardized transfusion reaction scenarios. [19] To our knowledge, our study is among the first to evaluate multi-agent LLM extraction of a standardized transfusion reaction feature list from real-world clinical notes.

Our findings provide useful insights for modern hemovigilance initiatives. Passive reporting systems are time intensive and often fail to capture most transfusion reactions. In one study of electronic surveillance, only 11 of 78 algorithm-detected cases of transfusion-related acute lung injury and 5 of 45 cases of transfusion-associated circulatory overload had been reported to the blood bank. [31] Structured, evidence-linked features extracted from clinical notes could be mapped to NHSN case definitions, severity, and imputability criteria, allowing transfusion services to identify potential reactions without relying on clinician recognition and reporting. Because each extracted feature is linked to verbatim evidence from clinical notes, outputs can be audited by transfusion medicine physicians or transfusion safety officers, expanding capacity while providing expert oversight.

Strengths: Our study has several strengths. Clinical notes were drawn from three sources that differed in style, length, and complexity, including expert-written scenarios, published case reports, and real-world hospital discharge summaries. All three models were open-weight, which ensures feasibility of local deployment in health data environments where sensitive data cannot be shared with external services. Every model output was independently assessed by two physicians using a blinded, randomized evaluation interface, with acceptable inter-rater agreement (89.4% observed agreement; Krippendorff's α 0.73), supporting the reliability of the reference standard. The feature vocabulary and extraction stages were pre-specified from the NHSN protocol before evaluation to ensure extractions were constrained and clinically useful. The pipeline decomposed extraction into single-purpose stages with evidence-linked outputs, allowing errors to be traced to a specific stage and feature, and all prompts and code are available for replication (Supplemental §4, §8). Automated extraction can reduce the manual chart review required to identify reaction features and is readily scalable to large clinical datasets.

Limitations: The pipeline assumed a single transfusion reaction per note and did not separate multiple transfusions, multiple reactions, or symptoms that recurred at different time points. Ambiguous or overlapping clinical descriptions were a source of error; for example, rib pain was coded as back pain, and hematuria and hemoglobinuria were among the most frequently missed features and can be refined in future work. Timing was more difficult to extract than clinical details, with strict timing accuracy of 81.8% to 88.7% across models. This is partly because we used only data from clinical notes, and retrieving laboratory and transfusion data directly from structured tables would yield more complete and accurate data on timing and transfusions. The models performed worst on synthetic clinical summaries, and the pipeline will require additional tuning prior to use with existing hemovigilance databases that contain only reaction summary data. This also applies to real-world notes that have not been pre-processed for research as MIMIC-IV notes have, and may carry duplicated content, formatting noise, and boilerplate text that may lack informational value and must be pre-processed prior to content extraction and use in AI pipelines. Physician validation of feature extractions was demanding, taking about 10-20 minutes per task, which limited the sample size of evaluations. Sensitivity was calculated from features physicians recorded as missing, and specificity was calculated among features the models reported; neither represents a classic diagnostic accuracy estimate. Each model was run once per note and on the lowest available temperature setting, however LLM responses show stochasticity. Larger models such as Kimi K3, while more accurate, also incur higher computational and environmental costs, which we did not measure.

Implications: Several potential extensions arise from our work. First, extracted features can serve as inputs to semi-deterministic pipelines for transfusion reaction classification, in which LLMs perform fact-finding and NHSN criteria are applied by rules. Second, the pipeline can support active hemovigilance by flagging notes with potential reactions for review by transfusion services and blood suppliers. Third, automated feature extraction can be used to code transfusion reaction features in large clinical datasets for research and as inputs to machine learning models. Fourth, the approach can be adapted for adverse event monitoring in clinical trials of blood products. Finally, future research should validate the extraction of treatments and past medical history, verify extracted findings against structured laboratory and vital sign data, and add reaction classification as the next stage of the pipeline.

5 | CONCLUSION

We developed and evaluated an LLM pipeline that extracts NHSN-defined transfusion reaction features from free-text clinical notes and found that open-weight models identified features with high precision, with omission of documented features as the main limitation. Our open framework provides a foundation for automated, auditable detection and classification of transfusion reactions to support active hemovigilance.

through application programming interfaces (Groq and OpenRouter) configured for zero data retention, with no use of submitted data for model training and no human review. PMC-Patients case reports and the Fung et al. scenarios are publicly available. As the study used only de-identified data, it did not require ethics review.

§1 Case-Selection Pipeline

Clinical notes were drawn from three sources.

1. MIMIC-IV hospital discharge summaries [21]

2. PMC-Patients published case reports [20] 3. Publication on AI for transfusion reactions by Fung et al. [19] (public domain)

For sources #1 and #2, we began by using a deterministic word search for the term 'transfusion-reaction-terms' and their acronyms (Table S6.1) to filter notes. This was followed by an LLM-based flagging for cases likely to contain a transfusion reaction. In a final step, the senior author SR manually filtered through all cases to select those with a potential transfusion reaction. The procedure is described in detail in §6 below.

All cases in source #3 were synthetically generated by the authors of that study for transfusion reaction evaluation and were thus potential transfusion reactions by design.

Additional details are provided below: 1.

MIMIC-IV discharge summaries. The de-identified MIMIC-IV note table (discharge.csv.gz) contains 331,793 discharge summaries, with the narrative read from the note's text field. Because the table is distributed as a single gzip-compressed CSV, it was streamed in fixed 5,000-row chunks and never decompressed to disk or held fully in memory, keeping the peak memory footprint constant regardless of file size. MIMIC-IV is used under the PhysioNet credentialed-access Data Use Agreement (DUA) and cannot be redistributed. 2. PMC-Patients case reports. PMC-Patients is an open-access collection of 250,294 patient case reports extracted from published PubMed Central articles, distributed as a single JSON array of approximately 837 MB. Before screening, the array was converted to a flat CSV by streaming it one record at a time, so that peak memory remained flat irrespective of the file's size. Every column was preserved; nested fields such as age (stored in the source as a list of value/unit pairs) were serialized to JSON strings so that no structure was lost and the original value could be recovered by parsing the cell. The narrative was read from the patient field. PMC-Patients is open access and carries no DUA. 3. Clinical scenarios from Fung et al study. The case set comprised 36 hypothetical posttransfusion adverse-event scenarios originally developed for a 2014 validation study of the CDC National Healthcare Safety Network (NHSN) Hemovigilance Module definitions. The scenarios were constructed to test application of standardized transfusion-reaction criteria and were independently classified by a transfusion medicine expert panel, whose determinations served as the reference standard. All but one scenario had a single intended classification; case 25 contained two simultaneous reactions, yielding 37 possible transfusion reactions across the 36 cases. Six scenarios intentionally did not satisfy the criteria for any NHSN adverse-event category and were therefore intended for classification as no reaction.

§2 Controlled Feature Vocabulary Creation

Case definitions for following reactions were extracted from CDC/NHSN Protocol Version 3.0

No. Reaction (as used to build the vocabulary) 1 Transfusion-transmitted infection (TTI) 2 Acute hemolytic transfusion reaction (AHTR) 3 Transfusion-associated circulatory overload (TACO) 4 Transfusion-related acute lung injury (TRALI) 5 Allergic transfusion reaction 6 Delayed hemolytic transfusion reaction (DHTR) 7 Delayed serologic transfusion reaction (DSTR) 8 Febrile non-hemolytic transfusion reaction (FNHTR) 9 Hypotensive transfusion reaction (HTR) 10 Post-transfusion purpura (PTP) 11 Transfusion-associated dyspnea (TAD) 12 Transfusion-associated graft-versus-host disease (TAGVHD)

A list of clinical features (symptoms, signs, clinical investigations, and syndromes) was extracted from case definitions. The features were then pooled and the list was deduplicated (e.g. 'shortness of breath' and 'dyspnea' were collapsed into dyspnea). A negative counterpart was then created by appending the phrase 'Evidence for absence of <feature> ', for example, 'Evidence for absence of fever'. This was done to allow us to explicitly instruct the model to detect pertinent negatives when mentioned, in addition to pertinent positives, during the extraction steps. Because some primary features extracted from the NHSN were already phrased in the negative, redundant double negatives were removed.

§4 Extraction Stages and Prompts

Each clinical note in the study cohort was processed in 3 extraction stages for the primary analysis, and 2 additional stages for exploratory work. All model outputs were structured JSONs. The model was instructed to quote direct evidence from each clinical note for each extraction.

• Stage 1, Extracting NHSN features. Given the note and the 181-feature vocabulary, the model returned each supported feature with its evidence span. The feature set was frozen after stage 1, and used in subsequent stages.

• Stage 2, extracting timing information for each feature. Given the note and the features extracted in stage 1, the model extracted temporal details. The model was instructed to express time in relation to the nearest transfusion when available, for example, "6.25 hours after completion of the apheresis platelet transfusion". Features with no attributable time are marked uncertain.

• Stage 3, extracting additional details for each feature. For each feature extracted in stage 1, the model was given the clinical note, the feature with its evidence, and that feature's menu of NHSN Hemovigilance Module variables, with the definition, response type, allowed values, and units of each variable. The model was called separately for timing variables and for details variables, and returned only the variables supported by the note; for example, "vomiting 90 minutes after the transfusion ended" was returned as an elapsed time from transfusion cessation of 90 minutes. Only variables in the feature's menu were retained. For pertinent negatives, only timing variables were filled by the model, and the details variable was taken from the stage 1 evidence.

• Stage 4, extracting treatments. The model extracted the treatments administered and, where stated, their response, timing relative to transfusion, and whether each treats a feature on the list.

• Stage 5, extracting past medical history. An independent stage that was given only the clinical note and listed pre-existing conditions, chronic illnesses, and allergies as a list.

Stage Input Output (JSON schema)

1: Features + evidence Note + 181-feature vocabulary List of {feature, evidence}; absences as "Evidence for absence of . . . ".

2: Time Note + numbered Stage-1 features {"times": [{"index", "time"}]}, one per feature; "uncertain" if none.

3: NHSN schema variables (timing and details)

Note + one positive feature (name + evidence) + that feature's Timing/Details variable menu {"variables": [{"variable_id", "value", "unit"}]} per feature per group, merged onto the feature as a nested schema object.

4: Treatments Note + all positive features (name + evidence + time) {"treatments": [{"treatment", "response", "timing", "feature_list"}]}.

5: Past medical history Note only {"past_medical_history": [{"condition", "detail"}]}.

Limitations: • Multiple transfusions are not easily separable in the current pipeline • Multiple transfusion reactions are not easily disaggregated • Multiple start/stops within a single transfusion are not easily detected or separately flagged Stage 1: Features and Evidence You are a medical coder.

You are given a list of features to look for in clinical notes. You return responses in JSON format. Analyze the clinical text below.

Extract features from the clinical text only if they match elements in the list. The features do not need to be phrased exactly as they are in the list, e.g. shortness of breath, heavy breathing, and difficulty breathing, can all be coded as dyspnea. If a feature is explicitly stated to be absent in the clinical text, return its negated "Evidence for absence of ..." form exactly as it appears in the list. For every feature you extract, also return the exact phrase from the clinical text that supports it as its evidence. Extract features and their evidence ONLY. Do NOT extract, infer, or return any time, timing, duration, or date information -that is handled by a separate step. Never include a "time" key or a "global time" entry.

Stage 2: Time

You are a clinical timeline annotator. You return responses in JSON format. You are given a clinical note and a numbered list of features that were already extracted from that note (each with its supporting evidence). Your ONLY job is to determine, for each numbered feature, WHEN it occurred according to the note. Do NOT add, remove, rename, or re-judge any feature. Do NOT look for new features. Do NOT consult any feature list. You only attach timing. For each numbered feature, report its time expressed WITH its anchoring context taken from the note -the reference event the time is measured from AND the direction (before / after / during / since) -NOT a bare duration. For example write "6.25 hours after completion of the apheresis platelet transfusion", not "within 24 hours" or "6.25 hours". If the note does not tie any time to that feature, return the exact string "uncertain" for it. Return JSON ONLY -no prose, no markdown -in this exact shape: {"times": [{"index": <the feature's number>, "time": "<time with anchoring context, or 'uncertain'>"}]} Include exactly one "times" entry for every numbered feature, using its number as "index".

Stage 3: NHSN Schema Fill-in (Timing Group)

You fill in NHSN Hemovigilance TIMING variables for one clinical feature at a time.

You are given: a clinical note, one feature found in it (with the exact evidence phrase), and a menu of timing variables for that feature. Each variable lists its definition, response type, allowed values, and (numeric only) a unit menu.

For each variable, decide from the NOTE whether it applies. Return ONLY the variables the note actually supports. Omit any variable the note says nothing about -do not guess.

How to write "value": -categorical -> exactly one of that variable's allowed values.

-numeric -> put the amount in "value" as a bare JSON number (e.g. 6.25, not "6.25") and the unit ALONE in "unit" (e.g. "hours"), taken from that variable's unit menu. -datetime/text-> put the date/time or phrase from the note into "value" WITH the context that gives it meaning. Never a bare date: write "6th May, 2 days after the transfusion", not "6th May". Example (ONE variable shown to illustrate the shape -a feature's menu may have several; apply the same rules to every variable the note supports): note "he started vomiting 90 minutes after the transfusion ended" -> {"variables": [{"variable_id": "elapsed_from_transfusion_cessation", "value": 90, "unit": "minutes"}]} Return JSON only: {"variables": [{"variable_id": "...", "value": "...", "unit": "..."}]} Include "unit" only for numeric variables. Use only what the note supports.

Stage 3: NHSN Schema Fill-in (Details Group)

You fill in NHSN Hemovigilance DETAILS variables for one clinical feature at a time.

You are given: a clinical note, one feature found in it (with the exact evidence phrase), and a menu of details variables for that feature. Each variable lists its definition, response type, allowed values, and (numeric only) a unit menu.

For each variable, decide from the NOTE whether it applies. Return ONLY the variables the note actually supports. Omit any variable the note says nothing about -do not guess. How to write "value": -categorical -> exactly one of that variable's allowed values.

-numeric -> put the amount in "value" as a bare JSON number (e.g. 6.25, not "6.25") and the unit ALONE in "unit" (e.g. "hours"), taken from that variable's unit menu. -datetime/text-> put the date/time or phrase from the note into "value" WITH the context that gives it meaning. Never a bare date: write "6th May, 2 days after the transfusion", not "6th May".

Example (ONE variable shown to illustrate the shape -a feature's menu may have several; apply the same rules to every variable the note supports): note "developed brisk epistaxis requiring nasal packing" -> {"variables": [{"variable_id": "verbatim_evidence", "value": "developed brisk epistaxis requiring nasal packing"}]} Return JSON only: {"variables": [{"variable_id": "...", "value": "...", "unit": "..."}]} Include "unit" only for numeric variables. Use only what the note supports.

Stage 4: Treatments

You are a medical coder. You are given a clinical text. You will extract the list of all treatments the patient received, the response to those treatments, the timing of each treatment in relation to any transfused products, and whether the treatment was given to treat a sign/symptom on the feature list. For each treatment, extract the treatment, medication, intervention, or supportive care provided; the patient's response to the treatment; when the treatment was given in relation to the transfusion or transfused product; and whether the treatment was given to treat a sign/symptom on the feature list. Use true for feature_list if the treatment was given to treat a sign/symptom on the feature list; otherwise use false. If the response or timing is unknown or unclear, record as "Uncertain".

A 31-year-old Saudi female patient with a known case of SCD presented to our ER [. . . ] with a history of generalized body ache, exertional dyspnoea, headache, easy fatigability and dark urine for a few days on a background history of infrequent (episodic) admissions for vaso-occlusive crisis but she was admitted 3 times to hospital over the previous 6 months because of anemia and thrombocytopenia (secondary to splenomegaly) and she received 6 units of packed red blood cells (PRBCs). Last blood transfusion was 18 days [. . . ]. On examination she was pale and sick [. . . ] abdominal examination showed splenomegaly (which was present since last admission). Investigations showed [. . . ] positive direct Coombs test and positive alloantibodies and there was no compatible blood for her for blood transfusion. [. . . ] The patient was diagnosed to have DHTR and so we treated her with prednisolone tablets 1 mg/kg daily, intravenous immunoglobulins, 0.4 gm/kg daily for 5 days, and rituximab 500 mg IV every week for 4 weeks in addition to folic acid 5 mg once per day. [. . . ] There was improvement in hemoglobin level in this patient with prednisolone, IVIG and rituximab and without being given a blood transfusion.

Stage 4: Treatments. Each cell gives the "feature_list flag" the model assigned whether the treatment targets a sign/symptom on the feature list; "-" means the model did not extract that treatment. All three models recorded the same response ("improvement in hemoglobin level") and an "after transfusion" timing for the three immunotherapies; they differed on folic acid, whose response qwen and kimi left "Uncertain" while gpt-oss-120b assigned it the same improvement. kimi recorded the fullest free-text response (the whole haemoglobin trajectory), and gpt-oss-120b appended the dose and the 18-day interval to each timing.

Treatment qwen gpt-oss-120b kimi Prednisolone true true true Intravenous immunoglobulins (IVIG) true true true Rituximab true true true Folic acid false true false Packed red blood cells (PRBCs) -true -Stage 5 : Past Medical History. Each cell marks whether the model listed the condition; "-" means it did not. qwen and gpt-oss-120b produced the same five atomic entries; kimi merged anemia and thrombocytopenia into a single entry and additionally listed the acute DHTR itself as history. Condition qwen gpt-oss-120b kimi SCD (sickle cell disease) ✓ ✓ ✓ Vaso-occlusive crisis ✓ ✓ ✓ Anemia ✓ ✓ merged* Thrombocytopenia ✓ ✓ merged* Splenomegaly ✓ ✓ ✓ DHTR --✓

§5 Output and Review Interface

Evaluators remotely accessed a private task inbox through a user pin, displaying only cases assigned to them. Opening a task presented the deidentified clinical note alongside three model-output tabs. The models were identified only as Model 1, Model 2, and Model 3, with their identities concealed and their display order randomized by assignment. Within each tab, extracted features were presented individually with the model-provided verbatim evidence quotation and any associated timing or clinical-detail information. Selecting a feature or evidence quotation highlighted the corresponding passage in the clinical note when a match was available. Evaluators determined whether each extracted feature was present or absent and assessed the accuracy of its supplied timing and other applicable clinical details. Pertinent negative features were evaluated as truly absent or present. Evaluators could add features missed by a model using a searchable NHSN-derived feature list and provide the expected timing or other required information. At submission, the interface checked that all required judgments had been completed for all three models, that required explanations had been entered, and that the evaluator had either recorded missing features or explicitly confirmed that none were missing. Figure S5.3: Evaluation task interface with feature details

§6 Candidate Case Identification from MIMIC-IV and PMC-Patients Cases

A deterministic regular-expression dictionary of twelve transfusion-reaction terms scanned the full corpus and retained any note matching at least one pattern, tagging each retained note with the names of the patterns it matched.

Table S6.1. Transfusion reaction search terms Full phrase Acronym (case-sensitive) transfusion reaction / transfusion-reaction(s) -Transfusion-transmitted infection TTI Acute hemolytic transfusion reaction AHTR Transfusion-associated circulatory overload TACO Transfusion-related acute lung injury TRALI Delayed hemolytic transfusion reaction DHTR Delayed serologic transfusion reaction DSTR Febrile non-hemolytic transfusion reaction FNHTR Hypotensive transfusion reaction -Post transfusion purpura PTP Transfusion-associated dyspnea -Transfusion-associated graft vs. host disease TAGVHD

On MIMIC-IV the screen retained 1,210 of 331,793 summaries (the full phrases alone found 843; the acronyms added 367). On PMC-Patients it retained 174 of 250,294 reports (114 from full phrases; 60 added by acronyms).

LLM Tagging

Because a keyword match is not proof of a reaction (a note may mention a reaction only to rule it out, or only in a past-history or checkbox line), each candidate note was provided to an LLM to determine to assign one of the following tags: genuine, ruled_out, or mention_only.

We used openai/gpt-oss-120b through Groq API for a stateless JSON-mode call with temperature 0 and reasoning_effort = medium. The completion-token budget was 8,192 tokens per call, escalated to 16,384 on a truncation retry. Identical configurations were used for MIMIC-IV and PMC-Patients. After model triage, the senior author SR manually reviewed candidate cases and determined inclusion in the final case cohort described in §1.

The system prompt is provided below.

You are a hematology chart-review assistant. You read one hospital discharge summary and decide whether it describes a genuine blood-transfusion reaction.

A "transfusion reaction" is an adverse reaction to a blood-product transfusion (e.g. red cells, platelets, plasma, cryoprecipitate) --for example febrile non-hemolytic reaction, acute or delayed hemolytic reaction, TRALI, TACO, allergic/anaphylactic reaction, hypotensive reaction, post-transfusion purpura, transfusion-associated dyspnea or GVHD, or a transfusion-transmitted infection. Classify the note into exactly one verdict: -"genuine": the patient actually had a transfusion reaction during this or a clearly described encounter --it occurred, or was clinically suspected and treated/worked up as a real event (transfusion stopped, reaction protocol run, symptoms attributed to the transfusion).

-"ruled_out": a transfusion reaction was considered and actively excluded, or the work-up was negative / not concerning / unremarkable. The phrase describes something that was checked and found NOT to be a reaction.

-"mention_only": the phrase appears with no actual clinical event in this note --e.g. remote past history, a consent or checkbox line, a generic risk or education statement, a differential listed but never pursued. Read the ENTIRE note before deciding; a reaction may be described only near the end. Judge only from what the note says --do not assume a reaction occurred just because blood was given. Return ONLY a JSON object with exactly these keys: { "verdict": one of "genuine", "ruled_out", "mention_only", "reason": a single concise sentence justifying the verdict, "supporting_quote": a short verbatim span copied from the note that best supports the verdict (copy it exactly; do not paraphrase) } Table S7.1d (continued) Feature Disagreements Evidence for absence of maculopapular rash 1 Evidence for absence of peripheral edema 1 Evidence for absence of positive DAT for anti-IgG or anti-C3 1 Evidence for absence of positive elution test with alloantibody present on the transfused red blood cells 1 Evidence for absence of respiratory distress 1 Evidence for absence of tachycardia 1 Evidence for absence of unexplained clinical illness consistent with infection 1 Flank pain 1 No pathogen is detected in the recipient 1 Oxygen saturation <90% on room air 1 Positive DAT for antibodies 1 Pruritus (itching) 1 Rapid fall in hemoglobin back to pre-transfusion levels 1 Respiratory distress 1 Most frequently missed and falsely reported features Primary reviewer, concordant occurrences. Missing rate = times missed / times present. Error rate = false positives / times the model reported the feature as present.

§3 Models and Parameters

All three models were accessed through programmatic, stateless API calls rather than interactive chat sessions. The same model was used for every stage of a given pipeline run. All calls used JSON output mode, so each response conformed to a fixed JSON schema. Responses truncated by the output-token limit were rejected rather than repaired. Transient and rate-limit errors were retried up to 10 times with exponential backoff (initial wait 1 s, factor 2, maximum 60 s). Outputs were cached per note so that each stage could be audited and re-run reproducibly.

Table S3.1. Model and inference parameters Model Provider Model identifier Temperature Reasoning effort Maximum output tokens Qwen 3.6-27B Groq qwen/qwen3.6-27b 0 Disabled* 16,384 GPT-OSS-120B Groq openai/gpt-oss-120b 0 Medium 50,000 Kimi K3 OpenRouter (Moonshot AI; provider fallback disabled) moonshotai/kimi-k3 1.0 † Low 20,000 *Reasoning was disabled for Qwen 3.6-27B because its reasoning output is incompatible with JSON output mode. †Kimi K3 is served at a fixed temperature of 1.0; this was the minimum available setting.

You return responses in JSON format. For example: { "treatments": [ { "treatment": "Lasix", "response": "Improvement in O2 saturation", "timing": "2 hours after transfusion", "feature_list": true }, { "treatment": "Diphenhydramine", "response": "Uncertain", "timing": "Before transfusion", "feature_list": true }, { "treatment": "Normal saline", "response": "Uncertain", "timing": "During transfusion", "feature_list": false } ] } <Positive Features List>

Stage 5: Past Medical History

You extract the patient's past medical history from a clinical note.

List every pre-existing condition, chronic illness, and allergy mentioned anywhere in the note -including the History of Present Illness, Past Medical History, Allergies, and Discharge Diagnosis sections. For each one, return: -"condition": the condition or allergy, in the note's own words.

-"detail": any qualifier the note gives about it (severity, trigger, status, etc.), kept close to the note's wording. If the note gives no detail, use "unavailable". If the same condition appears in more than one place, list it once.

Return ONLY a JSON object of this shape: {"past_medical_history": [{"condition": "...", "detail": "..."}]}

Example Extraction, Stage 4 and 5:

The note below was obtained from the PMC-Patients cases, describing a delayed haemolytic transfusion reaction (DHTR) in a sickle-cell patient. The note is lightly abridged for readability ([. . . ]); the model outputs are condensed into one row per extracted item with one column per model.

Reference standard reliability and analysis including discordant features

The two physician reviewers co-rated 1,343 feature occurrences and agreed on presence or absence for 1,201 (89.4%), with a Krippendorff's alpha of 0.73 (95% CI 0.69-0.77). The 142 (10.6%) discordant occurrences were excluded from the primary analysis. Table S7.1 repeats the primary analysis with all 1,343 co-rated occurrences (2,686 judgements, both reviewers pooled). The ranking of the three models was unchanged.

Figure 1.

Figure 1. Overview of the large language model pipeline for extracting transfusion reaction features from clinical notes. An example clinical note (top) passes through five stages. Each stage uses calls to the same large language model. Stage 1 extracts clinical features documented in the note. Extraction is restricted to a predefined list of 181 features based on the National Healthcare Safety Network (NHSN) Hemovigilance Module. Each extracted feature is marked present or absent, with a supporting quote from the note. Stage 2 adds the timing of each feature relative to the transfusion. Stage 3 converts features into NHSN schema variables. Stages 4 and 5 extract treatments and past medical history.

Figure 2.

Figure 2. Evaluation interface depicting a) case evaluation interface and b) case task list

Figure 3.

Figure3. False positives and false negatives per case evaluation, by model. Each dot represents one physician's evaluation of one case (50 cases, two reviewers each; 100 evaluations per model). Violins show the distribution of error counts; black lines mark the median. Only feature occurrences with concordant physician assessments are included. False negatives combine omissions and features incorrectly marked absent.

Figure S2.1.

FigureS2.1. Construction of the controlled feature vocabulary. Clinical features were extracted from the case definitions of 12 transfusion reactions in the NHSN Hemovigilance Module protocol, pooled, and deduplicated, giving 93 features. A pertinent-negative form ("Evidence for absence of. . . ") was added for 88 features, giving 181 features in total.

FigureS5.1: Evaluation home screen

FigureS5.2: Evaluation task interface

Model performance against the physician reference standard (concordant feature occurrences). Sensitivity and specificity with Wilson 95% confidence intervals.

Table 4.

Breakdown of errors by type for each model. Negated misses = the feature was recorded as absent; Omissions = the feature was not mentioned at all. Total false negatives = negated misses + omissions.
